On the same states, substituting code 0 changed the shoulder command by mean absolute 0.0070 (median 0), code +1 by 0.0217 (median 0), and code +2 by 0.0911 (median 0.0944). Thus the cue was not weak or clipped, and T6 was not wholly insensitive: it showed selective same-state sensitivity, especially to code +2. Sensitivity to the alternative feasibility codes 0 and +1 was small on average and zero at the median. This does not establish that any substitution is task-valid for the same target or would improve an episode. Together with M12's nine failures to hold and M11's aggregate tie, this limited diagnostic makes cue delivery alone an insufficient explanation and raises the priority of investigating learned approach/braking/hold behavior, while leaving whether stronger branch representation could help unresolved. | The I6 closure condition is met: M13 inspected M12 raw states using T6:checkpoint-90112's exact archived runtime and normalization, reported actual and counterfactual normalized cue values, and compared shoulder outputs with only coordinate 6 changed among all valid codes. It rules against cue clipping and against total policy insensitivity, but the diagnostic is restricted to ten selected failures (4,616 temporally correlated states) and its counterfactual actions are not episode interventions. No candidate or goal-level success inference follows. Preserve the unresolved distinction that selective cue sensitivity may be insufficient for feasible-branch choice, while repeated early-entry/short-hold and shoulder-stop failures motivate a goal-review decision about a learned stabilization/braking representation. | | I7 | closed | Can a learned policy explicitly conditioned on hold phase or hold progress learn approach-to-stabilization behavior that improves fresh full-distribution episode success over T3:checkpoint-115712 by reducing post-entry hold exits without sacrificing reach performance? | The human goal requires at least 196/200 successes on the official panel, but the best-known T3:checkpoint-115712 now pools 2,334/2,400 (97.25%) across six nonoverlapping development panels; the recorded predictive probability of passing a new official 200-episode panel is 35.15%. A policy representation that better manages the uninterrupted two-second hold is a plausible route to close that gap while preserving broad target coverage. | Complete and evaluate one learned hold-phase/progress-conditioned variant against T3:checkpoint-115712 on a fresh paired full-distribution 400-episode panel, inspecting paired episode successes and available first-entry, longest-hold/interruptions, and target-region diagnostics. Interpretation: the repeatable trace strongly supports a branch-feasibility representation failure as the upstream mechanism: T3's wrapped near-limit branch error becomes nearly zero at the stop even though that branch is infeasible, while the feasible alternative remains less aligned; the policy then drives the shoulder against its limit, saturates, and cannot hold. The rapid first entries in most episodes show this is not simply late approach. The evidence does not prove that branch choice alone causes every failure or exclude a secondary dynamic/hold limitation: M10 conditions on M9 failures, reuses the same deterministic seeds, and contains no intervention counterfactual. T4/T5's degraded marker-transfer implementations remain inconclusive about the broader mechanism because feature semantics, transfer learning, and normalization changed together. These results make a normalization-aware feasibility cue the next credible route, but do not establish that it will improve aggregate success. | M10, M9, M5, M6, M7, M8, E21 | M10 | Best-known remains T3:checkpoint-115712; no working candidate.
